← الفهرس · صفحة دليل

mlr(1)

الحزمة: miller

التراخيص: BSD-2-Clause

MILLER(1)   MILLER(1)

الاسم

Miller -- مثل awk، sed، cut، join، و sort للبيانات المفهرسة بالاسم مثل CSV و JSON الجدولي.

الملخص

الاستخدام: mlr [علامات] {أمر} [خيارات تابعة للأمر ...] {صفر أو أكثر من أسماء الملفات}

إذا تم توفير صفر من أسماء الملفات، يتم قراءة الإدخال القياسي، على سبيل المثال
mlr --csv sort -f shape example.csv

يمكن ربط مخرجات أمر بأمر آخر باستخدام "then"، على سبيل المثال
mlr --csv stats1 -a min,mean,max -f quantity then sort -f color example.csv

يرجى الاطلاع على 'mlr help topics' لمزيد من المعلومات. يرجى أيضًا الاطلاع على https://miller.readthedocs.io

الوصف

يعمل Miller على بيانات أزواج المفاتيح والقيم بينما تعمل أدوات Unix المألوفة على حقول مفهرسة بالأرقام: إذا كان هيكل البيانات الطبيعي للأخيرة هو المصفوفة، فإن هيكل البيانات الطبيعي لـ Miller هو خريطة التجزئة المرتبة بالإدراج. يشمل هذا مجموعة متنوعة من تنسيقات البيانات، بما في ذلك على سبيل المثال لا الحصر CSV و TSV و JSON المألوفة. (يمكن لـ Miller التعامل مع البيانات المفهرسة بالموضع كحالة خاصة.) هذه الصفحة اليدوية توثق mlr 6.15.0.

أمثلة

mlr --icsv --opprint cat example.csv
mlr --icsv --opprint sort -f shape example.csv
mlr --icsv --opprint sort -f shape -nr index example.csv
mlr --icsv --opprint cut -f flag,shape example.csv
mlr --csv filter '$color == "red"' example.csv
mlr --icsv --ojson put '$ratio = $quantity / $rate' example.csv
mlr --icsv --opprint --from example.csv sort -nr index then cut -f shape,quantity

تنسيقات الملفات

CSV/CSV-lite: قيم مفصولة بفواصل مع سطر رأس منفصل
TSV: نفس الشيء ولكن مع علامات جدولة بدلاً من الفواصل
+---------------------+
| apple,bat,cog       |
| 1,2,3               | السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
| 4,5,6               | السجل 2: "apple":"4", "bat":"5", "cog":"6"
+---------------------+
JSON (مصفوفة من الكائنات):
+---------------------+
| [                   |
| {                   |
|  "apple": 1,        | السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
|  "bat": 2,          |
|  "cog": 3           |
| },                  |
| {                   |
|   "dish": {         | السجل 2: "dish.egg":"7",
|     "egg": 7,       | "dish.flint":"8", "garlic":""
|     "flint": 8      |
|   },                |
|   "garlic": ""      |
| }                   |
| ]                   |
+---------------------+
JSON Lines (تسلسل كائنات في سطر واحد):
+------------------------------------------------+
| {"apple": 1, "bat": 2, "cog": 3}               |
| {"dish": {"egg": 7, "flint": 8}, "garlic": ""} |
+------------------------------------------------+
  السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
  السجل 2: "dish:egg":"7", "dish:flint":"8", "garlic":""
PPRINT: جدول مطبوع بشكل جميل
+---------------------+
| apple bat cog       |
| 1     2   3         | السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
| 4     5   6         | السجل 2: "apple":"4", "bat":"5", "cog":"6"
+---------------------+
جدول Markdown:
+-----------------------+
| | apple | bat | cog | |
| | ---   | --- | --- | |
| | 1     | 2   | 3   | | السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
| | 4     | 5   | 6   | | السجل 2: "apple":"4", "bat":"5", "cog":"6"
+-----------------------+
XTAB: جدول معكوس مطبوع بشكل جميل
+---------------------+
| apple 1             | السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
| bat   2             |
| cog   3             |
|                     |
| dish 7              | السجل 2: "dish":"7", "egg":"8"
| egg  8              |
+---------------------+
DKVP: أزواج مفتاح-قيمة مفصولة (تنسيق Miller الافتراضي)
+---------------------+
| apple=1,bat=2,cog=3 | السجل 1: "apple":"1", "bat":"2", "cog":"3"
| dish=7,egg=8,flint  | السجل 2: "dish":"7", "egg":"8", "3":"flint"
+---------------------+
NIDX: مفهرس رقميًا ضمنيًا (نمط أدوات Unix)
+---------------------+
| the quick brown     | السجل 1: "1":"the", "2":"quick", "3":"brown"
| fox jumped          | السجل 2: "1":"fox", "2":"jumped"
+---------------------+

خيارات المساعدة

اكتب 'mlr help {موضوع}' لأي مما يلي:
الأساسيات:
  mlr help topics
  mlr help basic-examples
  mlr help file-formats
العلامات:
  mlr help flags
  mlr help flag
  mlr help list-separator-aliases
  mlr help list-separator-regex-aliases
  mlr help comments-in-data-flags
  mlr help compressed-data-flags
  mlr help csv/tsv-only-flags
  mlr help dkvp-only-flags
  mlr help file-format-flags
  mlr help flatten-unflatten-flags
  mlr help format-conversion-keystroke-saver-flags
  mlr help json-only-flags
  mlr help legacy-flags
  mlr help miscellaneous-flags
  mlr help output-colorization-flags
  mlr help pprint-only-flags
  mlr help profiling-flags
  mlr help separator-flags
الأوامر:
  mlr help list-verbs
  mlr help usage-verbs
  mlr help verb
الوظائف:
  mlr help list-functions
  mlr help list-function-classes
  mlr help list-functions-in-class
  mlr help usage-functions
  mlr help usage-functions-by-class
  mlr help function
الكلمات المفتاحية:
  mlr help list-keywords
  mlr help usage-keywords
  mlr help keyword
أخرى:
  mlr help auxents
  mlr help terminals
  mlr help mlrrc
  mlr help output-colorization
  mlr help type-arithmetic-info
  mlr help type-arithmetic-info-extended
الاختصارات:
  mlr -g = mlr help flags
  mlr -l = mlr help list-verbs
  mlr -L = mlr help usage-verbs
  mlr -f = mlr help list-functions
  mlr -F = mlr help usage-functions
  mlr -k = mlr help list-keywords
  mlr -K = mlr help usage-keywords
أخيرًا، 'mlr help ...' سيبحث عن النص المطابق تمامًا '...' باستخدام مصادر
'mlr help flag'، 'mlr help verb'، 'mlr help function'، و 'mlr help keyword'.
استخدم 'mlr help find ...' للمطابقات التقريبية (نص فرعي)، على سبيل المثال 'mlr help find map'
لجميع الأشياء التي تحتوي على "map" في أسمائها.

Also see the File formats doc page, or # `mlr help file-formats`, for more about the file formats Miller supports. # In brief: # * For DKVP records like `x=1,y=2,z=3`, the fields are separated by a comma, # the key-value pairs are separated by a comma, and each record is separated # from the next by a newline. # * Each file format has its own default separators. # * Most formats, such as CSV, don't support pair-separators: keys are on the CSV # header line and values are on each CSV data line; keys and values are not # placed next to one another. # * Some separators are not programmable: for example JSON uses a colon as a # pair separator but this is non-modifiable in the JSON spec. # * You can set separators differently between Miller's input and output -- # hence `--ifs` and `--ofs`, etc. # Notes about line endings: # * Default line endings (`--irs` and `--ors`) are newline # which is interpreted to accept carriage-return/newline files (e.g. on Windows) # for input, and to produce platform-appropriate line endings on output. # Notes about all other separators: # * IPS/OPS are only used for DKVP and XTAB formats, since only in these formats # do key-value pairs appear juxtaposed. # * IRS/ORS are ignored for XTAB format. Nominally IFS and OFS are newlines; # XTAB records are separated by two or more consecutive IFS/OFS -- i.e. # a blank line. Everything above about `--irs/--ors/--rs auto` becomes `--ifs/--ofs/--fs` # auto for XTAB format. (XTAB's default IFS/OFS are "auto".) # * OFS must be single-character for PPRINT format. This is because it is used # with repetition for alignment; multi-character separators would make # alignment impossible. # * OPS may be multi-character for XTAB format, in which case alignment is # disabled. # * FS/PS are ignored for markdown format; RS is used. # * All FS and PS options are ignored for JSON format, since they are not relevant # to the JSON format. # * You can specify separators in any of the following ways, shown by example: # - Type them out, quoting as necessary for shell escapes, e.g. # `--fs '|' --ips :` # - C-style escape sequences, e.g. `--rs '\r\n' --fs '\t'`. # - To avoid backslashing, you can use any of the following names: # ascii_esc = "\x1b" # ascii_etx = "\x03" # ascii_fs = "\x1c" # ascii_gs = "\x1d" # ascii_null = "\x00" # ascii_rs = "\x1e" # ascii_soh = "\x01" # ascii_stx = "\x02" # ascii_us = "\x1f" # asv_fs = "\x1f" # asv_rs = "\x1e" # colon = ":" # comma = "," # cr = "\r" # crcr = "\r\r" # crlf = "\r\n" # crlfcrlf = "\r\n\r\n" # equals = "=" # lf = "\n" # lflf = "\n\n" # newline = "\n" # pipe = "|" # semicolon = ";" # slash = "/" # space = " " # tab = "\t" # usv_fs = "\xe2\x90\x9f" # usv_rs = "\xe2\x90\x9e" # - Similarly, you can use the following for `--ifs-regex` and `--ips-regex`: # spaces = "( )+" # tabs = "(\t)+" # whitespace = "([ \t])+" # * Default separators by format: # Format FS PS RS # csv ",” N/A "\n" # csvlite ",” N/A "\n" # dkvp ",” "=", "\n" # gen ",” N/A "\n" # json N/A N/A N/A # markdown " " N/A "\n" # nidx "\t" N/A "\n" # null "\x00" N/A "\x00" # pprint " " N/A "\n" # tabs "\t" N/A "\n" # xtab "\n" N/A "\n" # # --comment-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as a comment delimiter. # --field-separator-auto # Automatically detect the field separator. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --field-separator-regex {string} # Use the specified regular expression as the field separator. # # --field-separator-regex-auto # Automatically detect the field separator regular expression. This is the # default for most input formats. # # --input-field-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the input field separator. Same as # --field-separator. # # --input-field-separator-auto # Automatically detect the input field separator. This is the default for # most input formats. Same as --field-separator-auto. # # --input-field-separator-regex {string} # Use the specified regular expression as the input field separator. Same as # --field-separator-regex. # # --input-field-separator-regex-auto # Automatically detect the input field separator regular expression. This is # the default for most input formats. Same as --field-separator-regex-auto. # # --input-pair-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the input pair separator. # # --input-pair-separator-auto # Automatically detect the input pair separator. This is the default for # most input formats. # # --input-record-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the input record separator. Same as # --record-separator. # # --input-record-separator-auto # Automatically detect the input record separator. This is the default for # most input formats. Same as --record-separator-auto. # # --ips {string} # Input pair separator. # # --ips-regex {string} # Input pair separator as a regular expression. # # --irs {string} # Input record separator. # # --irs-auto # Auto-detect input record separator. # # --ofs {string} # Output field separator. # # --ops {string} # Output pair separator. # # --ors {string} # Output record separator. # # --ors-auto # Auto-detect output record separator. # # --pair-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the pair separator. # # --pair-separator-auto # Automatically detect the pair separator. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --ps {string} # Pair separator. # # --ps-regex {string} # Pair separator as a regular expression. # # --record-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the record separator. # # --record-separator-auto # Automatically detect the record separator. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --rs {string} # Record separator. # # --rs-auto # Auto-detect record separator. # # -F {string} # Field separator. # # -R {string} # Record separator. # # -s {file name} # Take command-line flags from file name. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # -x # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # # Miscellaneous flags: # # --comment {comment} # Add a comment to the output. This is intended for use when the output # is Miller's native format. # # --config {config file} # Read Miller configuration from the specified file. # # --exec {program} # Execute the named program, passing it Miller's output on standard input, # and printing the program's standard output to Miller's standard output. # # --from {file name} # Read input from the specified file. If omitted, Miller reads from # standard input. # # --headerless-csv-output # Output CSV without a header line. # # --headerless-csv-unless-வுகளில் # Output CSV without a header line, unless the input is comprised solely # of headerless CSV. # # --help # Print help message. # # --help-all # Print help message for all Miller commands. # # --help-csv # Print CSV-specific help. # # --help-dkvp # Print DKVP-specific help. # # --help-gen # Print GEN-specific help. # # --help-json # Print JSON-specific help. # # --help-markdown # Print markdown-specific help. # # --help-mlr # Print Miller-native-format-specific help. # # --help-null-terminated # Print null-terminated-CSV-specific help. # # --help-pprint # Print pretty-print-specific help. # # --help-tabs # Print TSV-specific help. # # --help-xtab # Print XTAB-specific help. # # --immediate-error-handling # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # --in-place or -i # Process files in-place. For each file name on the command line, output is # written to a temp file in the same directory, which is then renamed over # the original. Each file is processed in isolation: if the output format is # CSV, CSV headers will be present in each output file, statistics are only # over each file's own records; and so on. # # --in-place-delete or -i- # Process files in-place, deleting the original file. For each file name on # the command line, output is written to a temp file in the same directory, # which is then renamed over the original. Each file is processed in # isolation: if the output format is CSV, CSV headers will be present in each # output file, statistics are only over each file's own records; and so on. # # --in-place-rename or -i+ # Process files in-place, renaming the original file to have a .bak # extension. For each file name on the command line, output is written to a # temp file in the same directory, which is then renamed over the original. # Each file is processed in isolation: if the output format is CSV, CSV # headers will be present in each output file, statistics are only over each # file's own records; and so on. # # --json-error-values # Output JSON with error values represented as JSON nulls. # # --json-pretty-print # Output pretty-printed JSON. # # --limit {number} # Output at most the specified number of records. # # --log-level {level} # Set the logging level. Valid levels are: debug, info, warn, error. # # --log-quiet # Suppress all logging output. # # --log-verbose # Enable verbose logging output. # # --no-auto-categorize-json # Do not automatically categorize JSON strings as Miller types. # # --no-comment-strip {file name} # Take command-line flags from file name, like -s, but with no # comment-stripping. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # --null-terminated-output # Output null-terminated CSV. Same as --o--null-terminated. # # --only-field {field name} # Only output the named field. # # --only-fields {field names} # Only output the named fields. # # --output-time-format {format} # Specify the format for outputting timestamps. For more information see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/reference-main-flag-list/#output-time-format # # --page-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as a delimiter between pages of output. # # --page-length {number} # Use the specified number of lines as the page length. # # --page-prompt # Prompt before displaying each page of output. # # --peek {number} # Look ahead at the specified number of records. This is intended for use # with `mlr put` and `mlr filter`. # # --peek-all # Look ahead at all records. This is intended for use with `mlr put` and # `mlr filter`. # # --pprint-fields {field names} # Only pretty-print the named fields. # # --quote-all-csv-output # Quote all fields in CSV output. # # --quote-minimal-csv-output # Quote only fields which contain special characters in CSV output. This is # the default. # # --quote-numeric-csv-output # Quote only numeric fields in CSV output. # # --quote-style {style} # Specify the CSV quoting style. Valid styles are: all, minimal, numeric. # # --raw-input # Do not perform any input processing. This is intended for use with # `mlr from`, `mlr put`, and `mlr filter`. # # --raw-output # Do not perform any output processing. This is intended for use with # `mlr to`, `mlr put`, and `mlr filter`. # # --record-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as the record delimiter. # # --record-delimiter-auto # Automatically detect the record delimiter. This is the default for # most input formats. # # --record-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the record separator. Same as # --record-delimiter. # # --record-separator-auto # Automatically detect the record separator. This is the default for # most input formats. Same as --record-delimiter-auto. # # --seed {n} # with `n` of the form `12345678` or `0xcafefeed`. For `put`/`filter` # `urand`, `urandint`, and `urand32`. # # --silent # Suppress all output. # # --skip-comment {comment} # Take command-line flags from file name, like -s, but with comments # stripped. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # Normally this does not need to be modified, except when input is from # `tail -f`. See also # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/reference-main-flag-list/. # # --stdin-filename {filename} # Use the specified filename when processing standard input. This is useful # for diagnostics and for providing context in error messages. # # --suppress-output # Suppress all output. Same as --silent. # # --tz {timezone} # Specify timezone, overriding `$TZ` environment variable (if any). # # -I # Process files in-place. For each file name on the command line, output is # written to a temp file in the same directory, which is then renamed over # the original. Each file is processed in isolation: if the output format is # CSV, CSV headers will be present in each output file, statistics are only # over each file's own records; and so on. # # -n # Process no input files, nor standard input either. Useful for `mlr put` # with `begin`/`end` statements only. (Same as `--from /dev/null`.) Also # useful in `mlr -n put -v '...'` for analyzing abstract syntax trees (if # that's your thing). # # -s {file name} # Take command-line flags from file name. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # -x # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # # Output colorization flags: # # Miller uses colors to highlight outputs. You can specify color preferences. # Note: output colorization does not work on Windows. # Things having colors: # * Keys in CSV header lines, JSON keys, etc # * Values in CSV data lines, JSON scalar values, etc in regression-test output # * Some online-help strings # Rules for coloring: # * By default, colorize output only if writing to stdout and stdout is a TTY. # * Example: color: `mlr --csv cat foo.csv` # * Example: no color: `mlr --csv cat foo.csv > bar.csv` # * Example: no color: `mlr --csv cat foo.csv | less` # * The default colors were chosen since they look OK with white or black # terminal background, and are differentiable with common varieties of human # color vision. # Mechanisms for coloring: # * Miller uses ANSI escape sequences only. This does not work on Windows # except within Cygwin. # * Requires `TERM` environment variable to be set to non-empty string. # * Doesn't try to check to see whether the terminal is capable of 256-color # ANSI vs 16-color ANSI. Note that if colors are in the range 0..15 # then 16-color ANSI escapes are used, so this is in the user's control. # How you can control colorization: # * Suppression/unsuppression: # * Environment variable `export MLR_NO_COLOR=true` or `export NO_COLOR=true` # means don't color even if stdout+TTY. # * Environment variable `export MLR_ALWAYS_COLOR=true` means do color # even if not stdout+TTY. # For example, you might want to use this when piping mlr output to `less -r`. # * Command-line flags `--no-color` or `-M`, `--always-color` or `-C`. # * Color choices can be specified by using environment variables, or command-line # flags, with values 0..255: # * `export MLR_KEY_COLOR=208`, `MLR_VALUE_COLOR=33`, etc.: # `MLR_KEY_COLOR` `MLR_VALUE_COLOR` `MLR_PASS_COLOR` `MLR_FAIL_COLOR` # `MLR_REPL_PS1_COLOR` `MLR_REPL_PS2_COLOR` `MLR_HELP_COLOR` # * Command-line flags `--key-color 208`, `--value-color 33`, etc.: # `--key-color` `--value-color` `--pass-color` `--fail-color` # `--repl-ps1-color` `--repl-ps2-color` `--help-color` # * This is particularly useful if your terminal's background color clashes # with current settings. # If environment-variable settings and command-line flags are both provided, the # latter take precedence. # Colors can be specified using names such as "red" or "orchid": please see # `mlr --list-color-names` to see available names. They can also be specified using # numbers in the range 0..255, like 170: please see `mlr --list-color-codes`. # You can also use "bold", "underline", and/or "reverse". Additionally, combinations of # those can be joined with a "-", like "red-bold", "bold-170", "bold-underline", etc. # --always-color or -C # Instructs Miller to colorize output even when it normally would not. Useful # for piping output to `less -r`. # --fail-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # failing cases in `mlr regtest`. # --help-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # highlights in `mlr help` output. # --key-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # record keys. # --list-color-codes # Show the available color codes in the range 0..255, such as 170 for example. # --list-color-names # Show the names for the available color codes, such as `orchid` for example. # --no-color or -M # Instructs Miller to not colorize any output. # --pass-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # passing cases in `mlr regtest`. # --value-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # record values. # # # Pprint-only flags: # # These are flags which are applicable to PPRINT format. # --barred or --barred-output # Prints a border around PPRINT output. # --barred-input # When used in conjunction with --pprint, accepts barred input. # --right # Right-justifies all fields for PPRINT output. # # # Profiling flags: # # These are flags for profiling Miller performance. # --cpuprofile {CPU-profile file name} # Create a CPU-profile file for performance analysis. Instructions will be # printed to stderr. This flag must be the very first thing after 'mlr' on # the command line. # --time # Print elapsed execution time in seconds to stderr at the end of the # execution of the program. # --traceprofile # Create a trace-profile file for performance analysis. Instructions will be # printed to stderr. This flag must be the very first thing after 'mlr' on # the command line. # # # Separator flags: # # See the Separators doc page for more about record separators, field # separators, and pair separators. Also see the File formats doc page, or # `mlr help file-formats`, for more about the file formats Miller supports. # In brief: # * For DKVP records like `x=1,y=2,z=3`, the fields are separated by a comma, # the key-value pairs are separated by a comma, and each record is separated # from the next by a newline. # * Each file format has its own default separators. # * Most formats, such as CSV, don't support pair-separators: keys are on the CSV # header line and values are on each CSV data line; keys and values are not # placed next to one another. # * Some separators are not programmable: for example JSON uses a colon as a # pair separator but this is non-modifiable in the JSON spec. # * You can set separators differently between Miller's input and output -- # hence `--ifs` and `--ofs`, etc. # Notes about line endings: # * Default line endings (`--irs` and `--ors`) are newline # which is interpreted to accept carriage-return/newline files (e.g. on Windows) # for input, and to produce platform-appropriate line endings on output. # Notes about all other separators: # * IPS/OPS are only used for DKVP and XTAB formats, since only in these formats # do key-value pairs appear juxtaposed. # * IRS/ORS are ignored for XTAB format. Nominally IFS and OFS are newlines; # XTAB records are separated by two or more consecutive IFS/OFS -- i.e. # a blank line. Everything above about `--irs/--ors/--rs auto` becomes `--ifs/--ofs/--fs` # auto for XTAB format. (XTAB's default IFS/OFS are "auto".) # * OFS must be single-character for PPRINT format. This is because it is used # with repetition for alignment; multi-character separators would make # alignment impossible. # * OPS may be multi-character for XTAB format, in which case alignment is # disabled. # * FS/PS are ignored for markdown format; RS is used. # * All FS and PS options are ignored for JSON format, since they are not relevant # to the JSON format. # * You can specify separators in any of the following ways, shown by example: # - Type them out, quoting as necessary for shell escapes, e.g. # `--fs '|' --ips :` # - C-style escape sequences, e.g. `--rs '\r\n' --fs '\t'`. # - To avoid backslashing, you can use any of the following names: # ascii_esc = "\x1b" # ascii_etx = "\x03" # ascii_fs = "\x1c" # ascii_gs = "\x1d" # ascii_null = "\x00" # ascii_rs = "\x1e" # ascii_soh = "\x01" # ascii_stx = "\x02" # ascii_us = "\x1f" # asv_fs = "\x1f" # asv_rs = "\x1e" # colon = ":" # comma = "," # cr = "\r" # crcr = "\r\r" # crlf = "\r\n" # crlfcrlf = "\r\n\r\n" # equals = "=" # lf = "\n" # lflf = "\n\n" # newline = "\n" # pipe = "|" # semicolon = ";" # slash = "/" # space = " " # tab = "\t" # usv_fs = "\xe2\x90\x9f" # usv_rs = "\xe2\x90\x9e" # - Similarly, you can use the following for `--ifs-regex` and `--ips-regex`: # spaces = "( )+" # tabs = "(\t)+" # whitespace = "([ \t])+" # * Default separators by format: # Format FS PS RS # csv ",” N/A "\n" # csvlite ",” N/A "\n" # dkvp ",” "=", "\n" # gen ",” N/A "\n" # json N/A N/A N/A # markdown " " N/A "\n" # nidx "\t" N/A "\n" # null "\x00" N/A "\x00" # pprint " " N/A "\n" # tabs "\t" N/A "\n" # xtab "\n" N/A "\n" # # --comment-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as a comment delimiter. Lines starting with this # string will be ignored. # # --comment-delimiter-auto # Automatically detect the comment delimiter. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --comment-strip # Strip comments from input. Comments are lines starting with the comment # delimiter. # # --comment-strip-auto # Automatically strip comments from input. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --comment-strip-unless-input-is-from-tail-f # Strip comments from input, unless the input is from `tail -f`. This is the # default. # # --comment-strip-unless-input-is-from-tail-f-and-no-input-files # Strip comments from input, unless the input is from `tail -f` and there # are no input files specified. # # --comment-strip-unless-no-input-files # Strip comments from input, unless there are no input files specified. # # --comment-strip-unless-no-input-files-or-input-is-from-tail-f # Strip comments from input, unless there are no input files specified or # the input is from `tail -f`. # # --comment-strip-unless-output-is-to-stdout # Strip comments from input, unless the output is to stdout. # # --comment-strip-unless-output-is-to-stdout-and-no-input-files # Strip comments from input, unless the output is to stdout and there are # no input files specified. # # --comment-strip-unless-output-is-to-stdout-and-no-input-files-or-input-is-from-tail-f # Strip comments from input, unless the output is to stdout and there are # This is the default for most # input formats. # # --rs {string} # Record separator. # # --rs-auto # Auto-detect record separator. # # -F {string} # Field separator. # # -R {string} # Record separator. # # -s {file name} # Take command-line flags from file name. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # -x # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # # Miscellaneous flags: # # --comment {comment} # Add a comment to the output. This is intended for use when the output # is Miller's native format. # # --config {config file} # Read Miller configuration from the specified file. # # --exec {program} # Execute the named program, passing it Miller's output on standard input, # and printing the program's standard output to Miller's standard output. # # --from {file name} # Read input from the specified file. If omitted, Miller reads from # standard input. # # --headerless-csv-output # Output CSV without a header line. # # --headerless-csv-unless-வுகளில் # Output CSV without a header line, unless the input is comprised solely # of headerless CSV. # # --help # Print help message. # # --help-all # Print help message for all Miller commands. # # --help-csv # Print CSV-specific help. # # --help-dkvp # Print DKVP-specific help. # # --help-gen # Print GEN-specific help. # # --help-json # Print JSON-specific help. # # --help-markdown # Print markdown-specific help. # # --help-mlr # Print Miller-native-format-specific help. # # --help-null-terminated # Print null-terminated-CSV-specific help. # # --help-pprint # Print pretty-print-specific help. # # --help-tabs # Print TSV-specific help. # # --help-xtab # Print XTAB-specific help. # # --immediate-error-handling # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # --in-place or -i # Process files in-place. For each file name on the command line, output is # written to a temp file in the same directory, which is then renamed over # the original. Each file is processed in isolation: if the output format is # CSV, CSV headers will be present in each output file, statistics are only # over each file's own records; and so on. # # --in-place-delete or -i- # Process files in-place, deleting the original file. For each file name on # the command line, output is written to a temp file in the same directory, # which is then renamed over the original. Each file is processed in # isolation: if the output format is CSV, CSV headers will be present in each # output file, statistics are only over each file's own records; and so on. # # --in-place-rename or -i+ # Process files in-place, renaming the original file to have a .bak # extension. For each file name on the command line, output is written to a # temp file in the same directory, which is then renamed over the original. # Each file is processed in isolation: if the output format is CSV, CSV # headers will be present in each output file, statistics are only over each # file's own records; and so on. # # --json-error-values # Output JSON with error values represented as JSON nulls. # # --json-pretty-print # Output pretty-printed JSON. # # --limit {number} # Output at most the specified number of records. # # --log-level {level} # Set the logging level. Valid levels are: debug, info, warn, error. # # --log-quiet # Suppress all logging output. # # --log-verbose # Enable verbose logging output. # # --no-auto-categorize-json # Do not automatically categorize JSON strings as Miller types. # # --no-comment-strip {file name} # Take command-line flags from file name, like -s, but with no # comment-stripping. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # --null-terminated-output # Output null-terminated CSV. Same as --o--null-terminated. # # --only-field {field name} # Only output the named field. # # --only-fields {field names} # Only output the named fields. # # --output-time-format {format} # Specify the format for outputting timestamps. For more information see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/reference-main-flag-list/#output-time-format # # --page-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as a delimiter between pages of output. # # --page-length {number} # Use the specified number of lines as the page length. # # --page-prompt # Prompt before displaying each page of output. # # --peek {number} # Look ahead at the specified number of records. This is intended for use # with `mlr put` and `mlr filter`. # # --peek-all # Look ahead at all records. This is intended for use with `mlr put` and # `mlr filter`. # # --pprint-fields {field names} # Only pretty-print the named fields. # # --quote-all-csv-output # Quote all fields in CSV output. # # --quote-minimal-csv-output # Quote only fields which contain special characters in CSV output. This is # the default. # # --quote-numeric-csv-output # Quote only numeric fields in CSV output. # # --quote-style {style} # Specify the CSV quoting style. Valid styles are: all, minimal, numeric. # # --raw-input # Do not perform any input processing. This is intended for use with # `mlr from`, `mlr put`, and `mlr filter`. # # --raw-output # Do not perform any output processing. This is intended for use with # `mlr to`, `mlr put`, and `mlr filter`. # # --record-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as the record delimiter. # # --record-delimiter-auto # Automatically detect the record delimiter. This is the default for # most input formats. # # --record-separator {string} # Use the specified string as the record separator. Same as # --record-delimiter. # # --record-separator-auto # Automatically detect the record separator. This is the default for # most input formats. Same as --record-delimiter-auto. # # --seed {n} # with `n` of the form `12345678` or `0xcafefeed`. For `put`/`filter` # `urand`, `urandint`, and `urand32`. # # --silent # Suppress all output. # # --skip-comment {comment} # Take command-line flags from file name, like -s, but with comments # stripped. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # Normally this does not need to be modified, except when input is from # `tail -f`. See also # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/reference-main-flag-list/. # # --stdin-filename {filename} # Use the specified filename when processing standard input. This is useful # for diagnostics and for providing context in error messages. # # --suppress-output # Suppress all output. Same as --silent. # # --tz {timezone} # Specify timezone, overriding `$TZ` environment variable (if any). # # -I # Process files in-place. For each file name on the command line, output is # written to a temp file in the same directory, which is then renamed over # the original. Each file is processed in isolation: if the output format is # CSV, CSV headers will be present in each output file, statistics are only # over each file's own records; and so on. # # -n # Process no input files, nor standard input either. Useful for `mlr put` # with `begin`/`end` statements only. (Same as `--from /dev/null`.) Also # useful in `mlr -n put -v '...'` for analyzing abstract syntax trees (if # that's your thing). # # -s {file name} # Take command-line flags from file name. For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # -x # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # # Output colorization flags: # # Miller uses colors to highlight outputs. You can specify color preferences. # Note: output colorization does not work on Windows. # Things having colors: # * Keys in CSV header lines, JSON keys, etc # * Values in CSV data lines, JSON scalar values, etc in regression-test output # * Some online-help strings # Rules for coloring: # * By default, colorize output only if writing to stdout and stdout is a TTY. # * Example: color: `mlr --csv cat foo.csv` # * Example: no color: `mlr --csv cat foo.csv > bar.csv` # * Example: no color: `mlr --csv cat foo.csv | less` # * The default colors were chosen since they look OK with white or black # terminal background, and are differentiable with common varieties of human # color vision. # Mechanisms for coloring: # * Miller uses ANSI escape sequences only. This does not work on Windows # except within Cygwin. # * Requires `TERM` environment variable to be set to non-empty string. # * Doesn't try to check to see whether the terminal is capable of 256-color # ANSI vs 16-color ANSI. Note that if colors are in the range 0..15 # then 16-color ANSI escapes are used, so this is in the user's control. # How you can control colorization: # * Suppression/unsuppression: # * Environment variable `export MLR_NO_COLOR=true` or `export NO_COLOR=true` # means don't color even if stdout+TTY. # * Environment variable `export MLR_ALWAYS_COLOR=true` means do color # even if not stdout+TTY. # For example, you might want to use this when piping mlr output to `less -r`. # * Command-line flags `--no-color` or `-M`, `--always-color` or `-C`. # * Color choices can be specified by using environment variables, or command-line # flags, with values 0..255: # * `export MLR_KEY_COLOR=208`, `MLR_VALUE_COLOR=33`, etc.: # `MLR_KEY_COLOR` `MLR_VALUE_COLOR` `MLR_PASS_COLOR` `MLR_FAIL_COLOR` # `MLR_REPL_PS1_COLOR` `MLR_REPL_PS2_COLOR` `MLR_HELP_COLOR` # * Command-line flags `--key-color 208`, `--value-color 33`, etc.: # `--key-color` `--value-color` `--pass-color` `--fail-color` # `--repl-ps1-color` `--repl-ps2-color` `--help-color` # * This is particularly useful if your terminal's background color clashes # with current settings. # If environment-variable settings and command-line flags are both provided, the # latter take precedence. # Colors can be specified using names such as "red" or "orchid": please see # `mlr --list-color-names` to see available names. They can also be specified using # numbers in the range 0..255, like 170: please see `mlr --list-color-codes`. # You can also use "bold", "underline", and/or "reverse". Additionally, combinations of # those can be joined with a "-", like "red-bold", "bold-170", "bold-underline", etc. # --always-color or -C # Instructs Miller to colorize output even when it normally would not. Useful # for piping output to `less -r`. # --fail-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # failing cases in `mlr regtest`. # --help-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # highlights in `mlr help` output. # --key-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # record keys. # --list-color-codes # Show the available color codes in the range 0..255, such as 170 for example. # --list-color-names # Show the names for the available color codes, such as `orchid` for example. # --no-color or -M # Instructs Miller to not colorize any output. # --pass-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # passing cases in `mlr regtest`. # --value-color # Specify the color (see `--list-color-codes` and `--list-color-names`) for # record values. # # # Pprint-only flags: # # These are flags which are applicable to PPRINT format. # --barred or --barred-output # Prints a border around PPRINT output. # --barred-input # When used in conjunction with --pprint, accepts barred input. # --right # Right-justifies all fields for PPRINT output. # # # Profiling flags: # # These are flags for profiling Miller performance. # --cpuprofile {CPU-profile file name} # Create a CPU-profile file for performance analysis. Instructions will be # printed to stderr. This flag must be the very first thing after 'mlr' on # the command line. # --time # Print elapsed execution time in seconds to stderr at the end of the # execution of the program. # --traceprofile # Create a trace-profile file for performance analysis. Instructions will be # printed to stderr. This flag must be the very first thing after 'mlr' on # the command line. # # # Separator flags: # # See the Separators doc page for more about record separators, field # separators, and pair separators. Also see the File formats doc page, or # `mlr help file-formats`, for more about the file formats Miller supports. # In brief: # * For DKVP records like `x=1,y=2,z=3`, the fields are separated by a comma, # the key-value pairs are separated by a comma, and each record is separated # from the next by a newline. # * Each file format has its own default separators. # * Most formats, such as CSV, don't support pair-separators: keys are on the CSV # header line and values are on each CSV data line; keys and values are not # placed next to one another. # * Some separators are not programmable: for example JSON uses a colon as a # pair separator but this is non-modifiable in the JSON spec. # * You can set separators differently between Miller's input and output -- # hence `--ifs` and `--ofs`, etc. # Notes about line endings: # * Default line endings (`--irs` and `--ors`) are newline # which is interpreted to accept carriage-return/newline files (e.g. on Windows) # for input, and to produce platform-appropriate line endings on output. # Notes about all other separators: # * IPS/OPS are only used for DKVP and XTAB formats, since only in these formats # do key-value pairs appear juxtaposed. # * IRS/ORS are ignored for XTAB format. Nominally IFS and OFS are newlines; # XTAB records are separated by two or more consecutive IFS/OFS -- i.e. # a blank line. Everything above about `--irs/--ors/--rs auto` becomes `--ifs/--ofs/--fs` # auto for XTAB format. (XTAB's default IFS/OFS are "auto".) # * OFS must be single-character for PPRINT format. This is because it is used # with repetition for alignment; multi-character separators would make # alignment impossible. # * OPS may be multi-character for XTAB format, in which case alignment is # disabled. # * FS/PS are ignored for markdown format; RS is used. # * All FS and PS options are ignored for JSON format, since they are not relevant # to the JSON format. # * You can specify separators in any of the following ways, shown by example: # - Type them out, quoting as necessary for shell escapes, e.g. # `--fs '|' --ips :` # - C-style escape sequences, e.g. `--rs '\r\n' --fs '\t'`. # - To avoid backslashing, you can use any of the following names: # ascii_esc = "\x1b" # ascii_etx = "\x03" # ascii_fs = "\x1c" # ascii_gs = "\x1d" # ascii_null = "\x00" # ascii_rs = "\x1e" # ascii_soh = "\x01" # ascii_stx = "\x02" # ascii_us = "\x1f" # asv_fs = "\x1f" # asv_rs = "\x1e" # colon = ":" # comma = "," # cr = "\r" # crcr = "\r\r" # crlf = "\r\n" # crlfcrlf = "\r\n\r\n" # equals = "=" # lf = "\n" # lflf = "\n\n" # newline = "\n" # pipe = "|" # semicolon = ";" # slash = "/" # space = " " # tab = "\t" # usv_fs = "\xe2\x90\x9f" # usv_rs = "\xe2\x90\x9e" # - Similarly, you can use the following for `--ifs-regex` and `--ips-regex`: # spaces = "( )+" # tabs = "(\t)+" # whitespace = "([ \t])+" # * Default separators by format: # Format FS PS RS # csv ",” N/A "\n" # csvlite ",” N/A "\n" # dkvp ",” "=", "\n" # gen ",” N/A "\n" # json N/A N/A N/A # markdown " " N/A "\n" # nidx "\t" N/A "\n" # null "\x00" N/A "\x00" # pprint " " N/A "\n" # tabs "\t" N/A "\n" # xtab "\n" N/A "\n" # # --comment-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as a comment delimiter. Lines starting with this # string will be ignored. # # --comment-delimiter-auto # Automatically detect the comment delimiter. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --comment-strip # Strip comments from input. Comments are lines starting with the comment # delimiter. # # --comment-strip-auto # Automatically strip comments from input. This is the default for most # input formats. # # --comment-strip-unless-input-is-from-tail-f # Strip comments from input, unless the input is from `tail -f`. This is the # default. # # --comment-strip-unless-input-is-from-tail-f-and-no-input-files # Strip comments from input, unless the input is from `tail -f` and there # are no input files specified. # # --comment-strip-unless-no-input-files # For more information please see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/scripting/. # # -x # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # # Miscellaneous flags: # # --comment {comment} # Add a comment to the output. This is intended for use when the output # is Miller's native format. # # --config {config file} # Read Miller configuration from the specified file. # # --exec {program} # Execute the named program, passing it Miller's output on standard input, # and printing the program's standard output to Miller's standard output. # # --from {file name} # Read input from the specified file. If omitted, Miller reads from # standard input. # # --headerless-csv-output # Output CSV without a header line. # # --headerless-csv-unless-வுகளில் # Output CSV without a header line, unless the input is comprised solely # of headerless CSV. # # --help # Print help message. # # --help-all # Print help message for all Miller commands. # # --help-csv # Print CSV-specific help. # # --help-dkvp # Print DKVP-specific help. # # --help-gen # Print GEN-specific help. # # --help-json # Print JSON-specific help. # # --help-markdown # Print markdown-specific help. # # --help-mlr # Print Miller-native-format-specific help. # # --help-null-terminated # Print null-terminated-CSV-specific help. # # --help-pprint # Print pretty-print-specific help. # # --help-tabs # Print TSV-specific help. # # --help-xtab # Print XTAB-specific help. # # --immediate-error-handling # If any record has an error value in it, report it and stop the process. # The default is to print the field value as `(error)` and continue. # # --in-place or -i # Process files in-place. Same as --o--null-terminated. # # --only-field {field name} # Only output the named field. # # --only-fields {field names} # Only output the named fields. # # --output-time-format {format} # Specify the format for outputting timestamps. For more information see # https://miller.readthedocs.io/en/latest/reference-main-flag-list/#output-time-format # # --page-delimiter {string} # Use the specified string as a delimiter between pages of output. # # --page-length {number} # Use the specified number of lines as the page length. # # --page-prompt # Prompt before displaying each page of output. # # --peek {number} # Look ahead at the specified number of records. This is intended for use # with `mlr put` and `mlr filter`. # # --peek-all # Look ahead at all records. This is intended for use with `mlr put` and # `mlr filter`. # # --pprint-fields {field names} # Only pretty-print the named fields. # # --quote-all-csv-output # Quote all fields in CSV output. # # --quote-minimal-csv-output # Quote only fields which contain special characters in CSV output. Valid styles are: all, minimal, numeric. # # --raw-input # Do not perform any input processing. This is useful # for diagnostics and for providing context in error messages. # # --suppress-output # Suppress all output. Same as --silent. # # --tz {timezone} # Specify timezone, overriding `$TZ` environment variable (if any). # # -I # Process files in-place.
